A mobile inside a building finds its room by the point it stands on rather than by the cell the manager tracks for it, and of Jedi Academy's own sound marks the saber whooshes are played from the clips themselves while the body falls and the melee swings are still read and dropped, which is the one place a blow could come to sound twice if they are ever switched on.
2. A hull's rooms are hidden until someone boards; the proper fix is drawing them through the portal renderer with the windows as exits, so they show from outside. The same holds for a hull another player flies: its rooms are built here only when somebody asks to board it, out of sight and prepared before they are shown, and are hidden again the moment the walker steps out, so from outside a friend's ship is still a picture with nothing to see through its windows. They are dropped when that hull stops being drawn (a distance rule would be one number and one call site) rather than when it flies far off, and a peer's canopy stays solid while you are inside it, because the panes a ship's glass is swapped for are recorded when a hull is spawned and a picture has none.
3. The relay carries each player's vehicle (garage id, place, turn, the rider's pose and whether they ride, pilot or are aboard, and a winged ship's wings (open or closed), and whether it is set down on the ground, which stands it still on the other side instead of gliding it toward each message) and the figure's whole turn when aboard or adrift, drawn on the other side as a picture with no physics (`Garage.visual`); and whether they are hidden just now (`j`, in a hyperspace jump from its start until its tunnel opens, or in an ultra-fast cruise for the length of the run: the others hide them and their ship meanwhile and show them where they come out, since ten places a second a kilometre apart is a teleport on their side; the relay itself is unchanged and needs no restart for either); and each player's look and the weapons in their hands in their hello (`packLook` in `player/look.ts`, `held`), resent 400 ms after any change of clothes or weapon and put on the other side compiled before it shows (`applyLookPrepared`, `applyHeld`); and the ship they fly with its fit (`hello.ship`: a component per slot, the paint values and the droid, checked by `server/shipWire.mjs`), sent once when the Edit page closes or the ship changes and applied on the other side in place (a repaint, or the changed parts restaged: `RemotePlayers.refitRemote`); it does not carry the atmosphere crossings. Another player's ship and mount are silent: a peer's hull is drawn as a picture rather than made a machine the mixer knows, so none of its engine, wings or flyby is heard, although their blade now hums, ignites and hisses as a fighter's does, since it is drawn with the game's own renderer and that renderer is also the ear. The relay can also carry one word to one player rather than to the room (`ask`: a pilot asking another for room on their hull, and the answer), which is the first directed message it has ever had, and a ship clamped onto another player's hull is passed on as the hull it rides and the place on it, so the others draw it sitting still on that hull however the hull moves and the pilot sitting in it stays in their own cockpit; the relay must be restarted before either works. Emotes, dances and flourishes cross. A swing while moving or in the air plays on the upper body over whatever the legs do (Jedi Academy's run, walk, back-pedal, the angled strafe, the jump: `rig.overrideUpperOnly`), with the legs' angle and the torso's turn to the camera kept; standing, the swing has the whole body. Testing in a hidden Chrome tab: after five minutes hidden Chrome throttles chained timers to one a minute, so a test script's awaits stall; reload the page to start the five minutes again. The relay is now a small server as well, and the two are one program: `npm run relay` still starts it, a browser built before it still plays against it unchanged, and a browser built for it that finds an older relay at the other end says so and turns everything new off. What it holds is **one clock** — the world's wall time and how long a day is, handed out in its first word and sharpened by a round trip every ten seconds, which the time of day and the weather schedule both read, so two people on one planet are no longer at noon and at dusk at once — and **who each player is**: a key the browser makes for itself with `crypto.getRandomValues` and never sends, a public name that is a hash of it, and the characters that player owns, all written to `server/data/world.json` with a log beside it so a machine that loses power loses nothing. The same character opened in a second browser goes to the newer one and the older stops asking for it back; a character played with no server and then brought to one is settled by a change counter, the higher winning, with the question put to the player on a true tie. News goes only to the world a player is standing on, and a peer is taken apart on the other side only when their line actually closes. A join word on the address (`--word=`) keeps a stranger who guesses the port out, and damage between players is a switch on the server (`--friendly-fire`) that starts off and that nothing reads yet. **With no server address set none of this runs at all and the game is exactly what it was**, which the day's own test pins to the last bit. Everything about it is invented except the day's length (the client's 720 seconds until 2026-11-18, when the owner asked for 90 minutes of daylight: `GAME_DAY_SECONDS` 7,716 = the server's `DAY_MS` = the story's `GAME_DAY_MS`, held equal by `clock.test`, with the fast-forward key held to a whole day in `dayTune.fastDaySeconds`, 8 s, whatever the length): the protocol, how a player is named, the rates, the caps, the patiences and where each planet sits in the shared day. The server's own numbers are one `TUNING` object it prints back on `http://localhost:8787/` and moves for a run with `--set <name>=<number>`; the browser's are `__debug.session()` and `__debug.day()` (`__sharedDay()` is the same knob on the window). It holds **groups** as well: up to eight, asked in within the game's own 90 m, accepted or declined on a countdown, left, handed on, put out of and broken up, with the leader alone able to ask people in, remove them, hand the group on or offer it a trip. A member is keyed by their character rather than their line, so a reload keeps its place for a minute and a half and the group hears only that they stepped out and came back. The roster is its own light message that never reaches `RemotePlayers`, so a member on another planet costs this browser no rig, no clothes, no compile and no ship: it stands down the side of the screen and in the map's Group tab (the full stop, shown only while a server holds your group), is marked on the planet's map and on the space map, and a member past forty metres gets a chevron with their name over their head. And there is a **chat line**: Enter opens it, plain words go to everyone on your world and `/g` to your group, every line comes back from the server so that everybody reads the same words in the same order, and what anybody says floats over their head for a few seconds and stands in the message line. A player standing in a hull somebody else flies names that hull rather than sending a copy of it — the fix for two crew drawing two hulls on everybody else's screen — and that hull is now something you can really stand in: asked for one in particular, the browser builds that player's rooms for real (`src/net/remoteInterior.ts`), their own small physics world in the hull's frame with their own lights out of the pool that already exists, hung under the picture so they ride it as it flies and prepared and compiled out of sight before anybody is shown them. Nothing is built for a ship merely in view. You board one by walking up to it and pressing the same key that boards your own, or by stepping across at the way in from a hull of yours clamped to theirs, and E anywhere in their rooms is the way back out; the rooms step themselves once a frame whatever becomes of the player they belong to, because somebody standing in the ship of a pilot who has just dropped off the line is standing on a floor that has to go on being there, and a walker is always brought out before such a world is freed. Whoever leads a group can also take it with them: a crossing to another world offers the rest of the group the same trip with a countdown on it, and whoever says yes crosses their own way and comes out a step to one side of the leader, which is one word of its own (`cross`) going to the group and nowhere else. Still left of boarding and of travelling together: a passenger is not carried across by anybody's crossing (they are asked to step out first), nothing tells the other players that you boarded beyond the `in` field they already had, boarding from the gravity boots is not offered, two other players clamped to each other — neither hull yours — is not a crossing this browser offers, and the step to one side a follower comes out at is checked against nothing, so in space it can land inside something the arrival itself was chosen to be clear of. And it carries **the fight**: every player on your world has a body where they stand and a box round the hull they ride, both sensors in a collision group of their own that collides with nothing, so a bolt, a blade and a sweep stop on them while the steps you take, a ship looking for somewhere to set down, the roof over the rain and the ground under a speeder all pass over them; a shot crosses as an explicit message and is flown again on every other browser as a picture that hurts nothing and is cut short where the one who fired says it landed; the shooter's screen decides the hit, the one who was hit is the only place health is ever taken off, their health comes back at five looks a second and only when it has moved (which is what fills the roster's bars), and the one who died announces it with whoever struck the last blow; a blade turning one of those bolts away is a word rather than a second bolt; a peer's lightsaber is drawn, lit in the colour they chose, heard, thrown, and lights what is round it; and damage between players is the server's `--friendly-fire` switch, off unless it is asked for, with the game's own duel and peace inside it. Still left of the fight: a Force push, pull or grip does nothing to another player (the powers fire and the peer does not move), a creature or a fighter this browser alone holds (one stood from the console with no server's record, a follower) cannot hurt one, though one of the world's kept here can (its keeper's word to the player struck, `npcBlow`, which the server checks for reach and for whether that player's game has the creature at all), a peer standing in a hull's rooms cannot be shot (bolts fired aboard fly in that hull's own physics world and peer bodies live in the planet's), a ship's condition shares do not cross so two browsers each keep their own idea of how battered a peer's hull is, a homing rocket flies straight on every screen but the one that fired it, and a reflected bolt sounds like the plain blaster rather than the gun it came from. The world's own creatures are built: the server keeps what stands in a world and grants each one to the nearest browser within 180 m, changing hands only when a challenger has been a quarter nearer for three seconds; that browser runs its brain, its physics and its damage, and every other holds the same body `driven` (kinematic, brain off, eased toward a row four times a second, still shootable, still in the list of the living), with a blow struck there asked of the keeper, who is the only place health comes off. What the world's data stands -- the lairs, camps and nests, the people at their posts -- every browser stands for itself from the same rows, and those that may be fought go on the wire by saying they have been seen (the seen lesson above); an admin stands what everybody sees from the console, and can put a weapon in any hand already standing (`__debug.arm`). A hand-over carries the brain as far as it means anything on another browser: what it was fighting (as `p:<relay id>` or the creature's world name, never a key), its stun, slow and burn by the seconds left, and where it was walking; its grudges and a hold start fresh on the new keeper. The item ledger **is** built (`server/ledger.mjs`, wired through the relay; its browser half is `src/net/trade.ts` alone -- `src/net/owned.ts` is the creatures' keeper): the server keeps what each character owns, a row per thing under the name its browser minted, with its colours, and hands one thing from one player to another, name and colour going with it. Two of one shirt are two things to hold, show, trade and colour apart (the hail says `items: 2`; the relay must be restarted for it), and a thing's colours are its own (`tint`, by bare variable name): set on the appearance page for the copy worn, kept by the server (written lazily), carried by a trade, shown as a swatch in the backpack and the trade window, and newest-wins between this browser and the server. A job's reward still gives one of each, and nothing yet furnishes a house with *this* chair rather than *a* chair.
4. The lifts and terminals inside ship rooms are objects the server spawned, not part of the models. The shafts are cells named for them (`elevator1`, `reactorlift`, `empelevator`; `LIFT_CELL` in `lifts.ts`, which leaves out rooms named `elevator_room`) and have no floor at their upper levels, so a shaft's stops are its doorways: `liftStops` reads each portal out of the shaft (and out of any shaft it opens into, or is paired with by an `_up`/`_down` name) as a level, and E opens the lift menu (`liftMenu.ts`); a pick puts the player just through that doorway. No car moves, and stepping into a shaft from an upper doorway drops you to its bottom. A pick that changes level sounds where the player is put, one of the game's own elevator sounds going up and another coming down, which is our choice of when to play them rather than the game's. Buildings with no passable doorway from outside near the player (dungeons entered through server objects, stations with doors in the air) get an `E enter` that puts the player in the entry room (`doorlessNear`/`entryOf` in `layoutStream.ts`). The gallery's Interiors section labels the layouts with shafts.
5. Space: the zones convert and fly (`space` command; stations, asteroid fields, planets in the sky, the nebula skybox, hyperspace points and the jump scene; Kessel, Ord Mantell and Deep Space are systems of their own with nothing below, and what is made up in them is marked in the pack), reached from the top of a planet's sky and left through the ship menu (P; E aboard a multi-crew ship is for the controls and the door). Nothing but a station's own hull stops a ship flying at it (the zone's unbuilt ground at y -3000 did, with half the stations under it), and a boost in space asks the physics for no more than the 400 m/s it gives a body. A crossing carries the local player's place in the rooms and the ship's damage. A whole group crosses together now — the leader's trip is offered to the rest with a countdown, each member who takes it makes their own crossing and comes out a step to one side of the leader — but the relay still carries no crossing for a **crew**: a passenger standing in somebody else's hull is not carried with it and is asked to step out first, so a ship's crew still does not cross together. A jump is flown inside a tunnel of our own (the camera behind the ship; within a system the hull is moved in it, to another system the world is swapped under the hull, its rooms and crew carried across and walkable meanwhile); the others are told to hide the ship from the jump's start until its tunnel opens (the relay's `j`), a group jumps together (each member their own jump, arriving a step to one side of the leader), and a crew on the relay is still left behind — whoever is standing in somebody else's hull is asked to step out before they can take the trip. A small ship can be clamped onto a bigger one and carried on its back, the carrier's pilot flying both, with the crew crossing between the two ships' rooms at their own doors; a clamp onto another player's ship asks their pilot first, over the relay's one directed message, and the relay must be restarted for it; the step across at the doors works between your hull and theirs as well now, the first one building their rooms here. Flying into a carrier's hangar room, the other form, is not built. The System Map is a sub-panel of the ship menu (`hyperspaceUi.ts`), not a tab; the map's hyperspace-point rings are not built. Not done: landing at a station, flying the Star Destroyer from its own bridge (wanted later, the owner said), the stations' interiors (wanted as rooms in the same zone with the ship-interior technique, a still room in the station's frame, so ships show outside and players inside), the zones' nebulae now hang in the sky, strike by the game's own rule and weaken a ship inside them (neither seen by eye: whether the game's rare strikes or `bendWithin`'s old aim is wanted, and how strongly a nebula should weaken a ship, are the owner's to say), but how many sheets a nebula is made of, how big they are, how far in the far ones are pulled and what the haze looks like are all ours and have not been seen by anyone yet (every one of them moves live through `__debug.nebulae`), the fill they cost has never been measured in a visible window, and the sounds each row and each strike name are in the pack and nothing plays them, the space loading pictures (`texture/loading/space/`, which the `loading` command does not pick up), a made-up system of our own (`space_sandbox`, written by the `sandbox` command from a seed, 250 km across, everything in it invented and marked so) which has never been flown in a visible window and whose layout one number changes, the zone's own POIs on the map; all three stages of a jump sound now, and the warp effects' turn about the hull (`__debug.jumpFx`) is not yet confirmed by eye; on foot in space the player drifts with momentum (`evaUpdate`) but nothing yet needs a jetpack or a helmet, and the gravity boots that hold to a surface out there cut their patch out of one body's colliders only, let go rather than re-anchor once the walker crosses the edge of it, and name a hull for the aboard path's sake, so a zone with no ship in it has none; the planets' places and sizes come from the terrain file's PLAN forms and each body's `.pln` radius (read as a radius: no two bodies intersect in any zone and the close ones sit just outside each other, Ord Mantell's moons 3 to 34 m off a radius-50 planet, Endor and Yavin 4 about 125 m off their giants; diameter is not ruled out by the client); the game hangs every body at the same 2600 m, and overlapping bodies are drawn in their PLAN order (three sorts equal render orders by material id), which in every retail zone puts the nearer body in front (Yavin 4 over Yavin, Endor over its gas giant, three of Ord Mantell's moons over the planet); in space the star field and the star sprites are drawn in the opaque list before the bodies, so a planet covers the stars behind it, and a star group inside a body's disc never flares (`SwgSky.setSpaceOccluders`); the halos are not drawn.
6. The engine client data (`clientdata/ship/component/eng_*.cdf`, `GLOW` forms) carries glow sizes per engine style; their float layout is not decoded, so glows are sized by hull height.
7. Mounts wear the game's saddle on the creature's saddle hardpoint where it has one (the 22 the game let players ride, 36 with SOE's final names list) and a guessed one on the posed back otherwise (the top of the back's own vertices, never a neck held up over it); the rider leans with the saddle as authored (the tauntaun's and cu pa's 5 degrees back, the lava flea's 16 at its idle), and a tilt damping in `syncMount` is the follow-up if the owner finds it harsh. God rays are a depth-based radial pass in `postfx.ts` (sky is depth past 2.5 km; the sun projected to the screen; off with bloom off); the effects still wanted are listed in the README's "Effects to do". Moving things blur by their root's motion only: limbs, a ragdoll's fall and a peer's roll do not blur on their own (a previous-bone texture per skeleton is the sketched fix).
8. The map window (M) has four tabs, Here, Galaxy, Waypoints (Y) and Group (the full stop, only while a server holds your group), each key opening the map on its tab and shutting it from there, and M opening it on the tab used last: a planet's map is the client's `texture/ui_map_<planet>.dds` over the terrain's width (`maps` command, which also writes `galaxy.json` beside the packs: every planet's ground width and the game's own 30 shuttle routes with their prices, directed because the fare is not the same both ways, which the galaxy tab draws as the lines between the systems; where a system hangs in the galaxy is not in the archives at all, so the squares are canon and the layout round them is ours and marked as such wherever it shows); the space map draws the zone's own pack — its stations by the game's names, its hyperspace points, its launch point, its asteroid fields and its nebulae — as layers that switch off and on with the client's own zone-map icons, over the placed rocks and every ship; everyone in your group is marked on the planet's map and on the space map too, named, the leader's mark larger and inside a ring, switched by a box of its own that is there only while there is a group; it follows your ship until you slide the view and F brings it back, and a click names a place and offers the jump. The galaxy tab draws the galaxy itself, with a panel for the system picked and a jump into its orbit. Not done: other ships needing a ping, players you are not grouped with on either map, a member on another world (the strip and the Group tab carry their world instead), the docking lanes drawn, the System Map opening with the map's pick already chosen; the galaxy's star disc is a seeded spiral of ours rather than the client's own picture of the galaxy, the shuttle fares are read and not drawn, and the globes' pictures are about 15 MB uploaded into the map's own context and kept for the session. The pod racers' seats are the owner's table (`POD_SEAT` in `src/vehicles/podSeat.ts`: fg_8t8 and the IPG so far), else each pod's own `player` point, else its cockpit joint, hung under that joint so the pilot rides the pod's clips; the owner's `__debug.seat` pastes for pod_racer_one, two, Balta's and Anakin's are what to add next, and the gallery has to be converted again before the game's own point seats any pod (until then the joint's origin does, about half a metre from it).
9. The plain models' colour variants are whole GLBs beside the base (`mobiles/variants/`), and a combination whose palette none of the appearance's shaders bake comes out identical to the base and is not written: `shaderNeedsBake` looks at the first pass only unless asked for every pass, so a hue applied in a later pass never reaches the texture (the ships command bakes every pass of a ship's paint shaders and the wardrobe asks for `allPasses`; the mobiles' variants and their own wearables still use the first-pass check). Every `--var=name=value` on the command line is lost the same way for `sat`, `parts`, `player` and `species`, because the option parser splits at every `=` (`cli.mjs`); the `mobiles` command passes its values as a Map and is not affected.
10. Weather: a storm's own bed plays from the sky's rows, but the thunder its rain sheets carry is off by default so that the two do not double (`__debug.ambience({ weatherParticles: true })` adds it), and the owner's ear has not settled that yet; the climate weights and Life Day's dates are invented; weather writes no depth, so ambient occlusion and depth of field treat rain as whatever is behind it; Mustafar's lightning (a chain of eleven carried effects) plays, but its look has not been seen yet; props inside buildings share materials with the same props outdoors, so seen through a door from beyond the roof grid (about 36 m) they can show a faint wet sheen.
11. The backpack: species cuts are recorded, not converted, for the shared human folders (a Rodian wears the human hat mesh; `fit.own` names its cut), and the species packs' own dress (shirt, trousers, shoes) overrides the appearance table for the species it blocks. NPC fighters still pick outfits by the give tab's regex groups and ignore `fit`, so a Wookiee fighter can wear a blocked shirt. Item modifiers (armour, damage variability) are not modelled; nothing limits how much is carried.
12. Sound: the game is no longer silent, and it now has music -- **only the music players make**. Hold one of the game's instruments (they come through the weapons pack, since that is what puts a model in a hand) and a row under the world offers the songs that instrument has a part in, a play button and its eight flourishes; what sounds is the game's own track for that instrument, and standing beside somebody on another instrument is the two halves the game wrote, lined up off the clock everybody shares with nothing sent between browsers. The background score is deliberately left unconverted and always will be. Still to do there: another player's performance does not cross the relay yet (your own instrument and anybody you can already hear in the same browser are what work), no animation plays while you play, and the samples want the `music` command run with them (it is about 100 MB). Working: the mixer and the game's own bank behind a Sound page (a master, a slider a layer, and switches for headphones, the room echo, keeping the sound when the tab goes and which saber set), the world's own beds, rooms, placed sound objects and particle loops, every body's feet and voice, the guns, fists, blades and grenades, the blades' own hum and whoosh, the wall a sound is heard through and the echo of the room it stands in, and every machine's engine, wings, flyby, water, hits, jump, lift and room hum. Still silent: no music plays at all (the score and the instrument parts are left out of the bank on purpose, and the eighteen music templates it does keep are the ones a world places in a room); another player's ship or mount over the relay, since a peer's hull is a picture rather than a machine the mixer knows — their lightsaber is heard now, because it is drawn with the game's own blade renderer and that renderer is also the ear, which nothing asked for and nothing forbade, so say the word if several peers with blades out are too much; the boosters' own rocket loop, which is read and never played; a damaged ship, whose damage loops are read and never chosen between; two hulls and six of the gallery's vehicles, which have no engine sound in the archives at all; the nebulae's rows and each strike's sound; and the thousands of spoken lines in the bank, since nothing in the game talks. Ours rather than the game's, and all of it tunable live: how much of a room's echo a sound takes and how long the two echoes are, how much a wall takes off a sound, which of the game's four elevator sounds a pick plays, which of the three power chimes a hull answers to, the idle, speed-up and slow-down loop beside a run loop, and every number in `MIXER_TUNE`, `DISTANCE_TUNE`, `VEHICLE_TUNE`, `FOOT_TUNE`, `GUN_TUNE` and `SABER_TUNE`, through `__debug.audio`, `vehicleSounds`, `footsteps`, `surface`, `gunSounds` and `sabers`, none of which can be heard from a driven tab and all of which answer in numbers.
13. The interface: the whole screen is ours now and none of the client's own interface art is used anywhere. Built: the palette, the overlay canvas and the geometry table under everything; the flight display (the reticle, the three arcs, the ship's condition with its pips, the target's bracket and its edge arrow); the message line; the sheet of marks we drew by hand and the on-foot bars, ability cells, hands and crosshair that wear them; the four-slot action bar, whose state is gathered eight times a second into one struct that the long prompt line reads too; the name and health over whatever the crosshair rests on; the damage arc, the hit tick and the numbers over heads; the Interface page in the Escape menu; the minimap as a bare circle carrying the planet's name, the map window's own coordinates and a north mark, written only when the rounded pair moves; and every panel's colours moved onto the same eighteen names. The journal is on J and the pick-up on O, and a key a player had already chosen for something else stays theirs. Not done: the radar, which was the optional third wave and is not started. Loose ends worth knowing. The wing states and the message line's kinds have marks drawn for them that nothing shows, because `src/ui/shipHud.ts` and `src/ui/messages.ts` were not opened in that wave; two lines each when they next are. `hudMath`’s layout struct is still informational -- the flight display places its own blocks and the message line is placed by `hud.css` -- and `--hud-msg-w` is not written, so a long message line is clamped by the stylesheet’s own fallback. The bar holds four actions, so on a winged fighter with guns `next target` falls off the end, a ship's burn is deliberately not offered (it is a key held everywhere, not an action taken at a place), and standing in a lift shaft at a bridge's controls the bar offers the ship while the long line offers the lift. With the message line switched off the NPC pilots’ taunts go with it. A blow a body refuses for a reason other than being dead (an ally’s, which nothing outside the body can see) still ticks the crosshair, and a blow on a ship or a turret does not tick it at all, since the hook watches the living. The hit tick is four marks of its own just outside the crosshair rather than the crosshair’s own ticks jumping outward, because the two crosshairs belong to files that wave did not open. Aboard a ship's rooms a bolt's arc can point the wrong way, since the bolt's place is the hull's frame and the camera’s axes are the world’s. A number rises from `pos.y + halfHeight`, which is the head on anything measured from the feet and the middle on anything measured from its centre. The map's own canvas colours (`mapUi.ts`) are the last place in the game where a palette value has a second home: a 2D context takes strings, so they want `colourOf(COL.…)` from `src/core/palette.ts` rather than a `var()`. `App` still keeps a wrapper round the player's damage record as a second source for the direction of a blow, which the record itself now carries; it costs nothing and can go whenever that line and its check are changed together. And nothing of any of it has been seen from a driven tab: how it looks, whether each of the marks can be read at a glance, whether a hairline reads over a noon dune and over a starfield alike, whether the wings' amber span and the boost's cyan one read the right way round, whether the short bar has hidden something the owner uses, and what the overlay really costs are all the owner’s to judge.

14. Homes, props and what a world stands for good. A building can be put down in the world you are standing in and walked into, out of the gallery pack, on the ground as it finds it, and so can any of the game's props (`__debug.house`, `__debug.homes`, `__debug.prop`). **Yours are kept by a server or by this machine**: with a server they are real -- the relay writes your houses, and once restarted for it your props, down beside the characters and the items, everybody on that world sees everybody's, everybody arriving later is handed them, and they outlast a restart or a power cut -- and with none both are kept on this machine per world, shared by every character on this browser, and stand again the next time anybody comes back with no server. A line that drops keeps the server's things standing and refuses changes until it is back. **Everything in hand is placed the same way**: it appears out where the camera looks; R held with the mouse moving up and down turns it and F held raises or lowers it (a slow hand a little, a quick one a long way), Shift with R tips and rolls a prop, the wheel with a key held steps to the grid and the wheel alone pushes it out and pulls it in; a click puts it down, Escape gives it up without opening the menu, Delete gives up a deed or a prop taken fresh and throws one picked back up away; and nothing fires, swings or throws while something is in hand. O picks your nearest prop up exactly where it stood, and at your own door picks the house itself up the same way, so a house is moved rather than built again (a server's moves when the server answers, and only on a server restarted for it). The ground is refused in words when it stands too far over the doorstep, falls too far away or tilts too much, judged at the house's floor and on the ground it really stands on whichever way it is turned; a door left high is a note and never a refusal. A spot is refused when the world already has something standing there or when it is under water, and a prop of yours within a story placement's guard. **A building is put down from a deed, by hand**: the Housing tab lists the 47 buildings the archives and the emulator between them let a player buy, with the game's own names, sizes, lot counts and daily upkeep, and a double-click takes one in hand with the client's own footprint grid under it, green or red. **Permanent placements**: every world stands, for every player, the rows of two files that ship with the build, `public/placements/story/<world>.json` (the story's dressing) and `public/placements/world/<world>.json` (map edits); a row reaches players once its file is committed and merged to main, stands solid and guarded as part of the world (O says so and never picks one up), and a quest names one as `"template": "place/<id>"`, which `npm run story:check` checks against both files. The world edits committed so far are the old tutorial's world's and Monument's. A world's ground heights ship the same way since Map-maker II's first wave, `public/placements/heights/<world>.json`, written only by the world editor's sculpt brush (K) and read by every browser at the arrival, and so does its paint since the second wave, `public/placements/paint/<world>.json` (the families and colours painted in 4 m cells, the palette and the world's own row), written only by the paint brush (U), and its lakes since the fifth and its rivers since the sixth, `public/placements/water/<world>.json` (lakes of ours in the raw frame, each a level and an outline, and rivers, each a spine of points with their surfaces, a width and a velocity), written only by the water tool (Z), and its places since the eighth, `public/placements/locations/<world>.json` (cities, places, landmarks, areas and zones of ours, each a shape in the raw frame with its no-build switch and loading lines, and since the ninth wave a family, a weather kind and a grade look of its own; and the world row's own lines and `cities: 'noBuild'`, and since the ninth its family, weather kind and grade under every place's and its climate, fog, wind and clouds), written only by the places tool (P); and since the twelfth, what a world takes away, `public/placements/removed/<world>.json` (one of its own objects hidden or moved by its content key, a piece of a building's own furniture in one copy, a thing's or this world's model's walk-through switch, a person by key, a lair's site, a no-spawn zone), and his own people, `public/placements/people/<world>.json`, both written only by the world editor (its World's own filter, the library's People source and the zone tool) and the shared walk-through file `solid.json` by the editor too; and since the thirteenth the looks his people wear, `public/placements/looks/all.json`, one file every world shares, written only by the world editor's dresser (its Looks tab) and merged into the creature catalogue at every boot as bodies of their own (`own:<id>`) that a placed person or a story cast member names; and since the wave of the rooms' lights the rooms' lights, `public/placements/lights/<world>.json` (one copy's and this world's) and the shared `public/placements/lights.json` (every copy everywhere), written only by the world editor's lights tool (;) and read by every browser at the arrival, never waited for; Monument carries a heights and a paint file and no world a water, removal or people file, and there is no looks file, yet, and the outdoor walk grid follows a world's heights, its lakes and rivers (the terrain's own rivers too), its solid permanent rows and its removal file's hides and switches once `navgrid <world>` is run again, which `status` asks for whenever one of them moves (a real-elevation world has no walk grid). On the dev server alone they are written from inside the game, by the world editor (F2, above) and by the Props and Housing tabs' Yours or Permanent switch, L in hand for which file, a label at the top saying where things go, the first save to a file each session on a second click, O on a permanent row to move it, and a Standing here list with Go to, Pick up, Delete, Rename, a name and a note, Solid, and a story object or an area to paste; `__debug.storyHere()` hands over the same two beside the nearest row. A permanent building row stands its template's own furniture beside it (wave ILF) unless it says `furnish: false`, and a deed's house or a home never does. **None of it has been seen by eye**, here or on the relay, because the tabs it was written in draw no frames: not the hold to turn and raise, the label, the editor's list, a house moved, a server's props, or a permanent row standing in a town. What is not built: nothing costs anything or counts against a lot (the numbers are read and shown and nothing enforces them), no maintenance is taken, there is no permission list (no-build zones exist since Map-maker II's eighth wave: a place of ours with `build: false` refuses a player's deed, house and prop in its own words, and the game's own cities, Core3's no-build areas and the client's no-build radii do too under a world's `cities: 'noBuild'` switch, off by default; a server checks none of it), and nobody but its owner and the admin may change a thing of yours. Known to be missing rather than undone: a house put down from the console or by its model alone is judged on its model's box (a deed's footprint judges the rest); the procedural flora is kept off a patch only on chunks built after it was placed, so a house put down in a wood stands in the wood, and so does a permanent row added while its chunks already stand; a moved house leaves what was put down inside it where it stood; nobody stands in a permanent building (a row's `fill` is read and set aside), its doors are never locked (`locked`, the same), and the outdoor walk grid knows it is there only once `navgrid` is run again for its world (`status` asks); every permanent row filed before its region's tier is built (an arrival's, whose regions wait for them) is folded into that tier with the snapshot's own objects, while one stood in play stands on meshes of its own until the next arrival or the world editor's Fold now (a file's backstop is 100,000 rows, where it was 2,000); rows out of a world's own snapshot pack, or another world's (`from`), and a row's `scale`, its looks (`mats`: a material tinted, or given another model's picture or material), what hangs on it (`attach`: effects and sounds in its own frame), a mark (`kind: 'mark'`, a row of effects and sounds with no model) and a row of one of the owner's own models (`pack: 'imported'`, made by `npm run swg -- import` and stood out of `public/imports/` where that holds the model, else the dev server's git-ignored `imports-private/`, which no build carries: a row of a model never shipped stands only on the dev server, and a house of his with rooms is walked into, lit, floored and doored as a converted building is) are written by the world editor alone, which writes many rows in one ask (the endpoint's `batch`) where the tabs write one; this machine's props are never handed up to a server, and its houses are this browser's rather than a character's; the story workspace's reference builder does not know `place/` ids yet, which is a story session's to teach it; and **story dressing does not come from a server**: a story played on somebody's server is dressed by what each player's own build carries, so the owner's dressing must be committed here to be seen by anybody.

15. Travel: **the shuttles run, from the terminals the game put them behind**. Walk into a starport, use the travel terminal standing in it, and a window shows this world's ports on this world's own map with the fare from where you stand; its galaxy side picks another world first and then a port on it. Purchase takes the fare out of your credits and puts a ticket in hand, and the ticket is handed to the ticket collector outside -- which will only let you board while a shuttle is really there, on a clock every browser shares. The terminals, the collectors and the shuttles are where the game's own building templates put them, 189 of them over 11 worlds. The shuttles now land, wait and lift off on that clock, each on the game's own rig with its own clips (the transports fold their struts and open their doors), are solid while they wait, and hum, fire their engines, raise smoke on the pad and sound their landings and take-offs at the moments their own clips mark; how the effects look has not been seen by eye, nor whether the pieces sit right on their joints, whether each pad's approach clears the town round it, or whether the shuttle's lift-off, which ends 67 m up and is then simply gone, wants a fade. **A ticket is flown now.** Handed to the collector it seats you, out of sight, in the shuttle on the pad, which lifts off on that clock and on its own clip; a ticket about this world is flown there by the shuttle's own pilot and landed on the far pad's own clip, and a ticket to another world, with the terminal's box left unticked, flies the whole way -- out of the sky past the space gate, up into the orbit, the jump every ship makes (or, between Corellia and Talus or Naboo and Rori, a flight across the system), a turn toward the far world's disc, down to a point high over the far pad's approach, and in to land -- while with the box ticked (always, for Mustafar, which has no orbit) it is carried across under a loading screen straight onto the far pad's landing. A port with no rigged pad (six of the game's, the two Restuss ports of the ruins among them; Kachirho's has SOE's own transport since the map fixes' second wave) sets you down on foot, a trip that cannot go on is skipped or sets you down at the port, and nothing leaves anybody in space. One whole trip through space, Corellia to Tatooine, has been flown in a driven tab with no errors; none of it has been seen by eye -- the swap on the pad, the blends at the cut and the join, the chase view of a 43 m hull, the jump from a passenger's seat, a transport landing on a shuttleport's pad. Still not built of it: nobody else sees you ride (you are hidden from the others for the whole trip), and a trip has no stops on the way (Kachirho's starport and Mustafar's Mensix facility have SOE's own terminal, collector and transport since the map fixes' second wave, and no trip from either has yet been flown). One pad is known to be flown badly: onto Naboo's Lake Retreat Shuttleport, from another port or down from space, a ridge up to 375 m over the pad stands on the landing's own line behind its join, so the pilot dives at up to 35 degrees, goes round once and still reaches the landing 34 m (a transport) to 164 m (a shuttle) too high; the real-ground script names it on every run (`KNOWN_HIGH` in `tools/swg/tests/shuttleCourseTerrain.ts`) and fails on any other crossing down that does the same. What is not built: a ticket does not survive a logout, nothing is refused for being the wrong faction or over a lot limit, and the developer's own `__debug.shuttle({ list: true })` still travels with no ticket at all. Nothing of it has been seen by eye.
